A lot indeed: the protocol is at v3.111 now, with two skill bumps from this evening. Let me read your run record and check it against the repos.

Everything you ran landed, and the repos agree with your record: the orrery is at b755415 with your run record pushed after it, the website at b1a4ce6. Here's where that leaves things.

**Done tonight, verified.** Reset is the one button in every room, and your phone look said "correct." Slice 2's orrery half is built: the path plan read JPL's real positions for all twelve spacecraft and found the flybys where the missions actually made them, Voyager 2 at Neptune at 29,000 km, New Horizons at Pluto at 13,680 km, Juno's Earth pass. C/2025 K1 is asked by JPL's permanent number again and your look confirmed it sits with its pieces. Two skills bumped and reinstalled, protocol at v3.111. Gonggong's period came through the maintenance run as yesterday's ruling intended.

**Not yet captured.** The session hasn't closed: the Where We Are page at HEAD still says "paste the preview's flyby list," which you did. Four things of yours exist only in the run record:

1. The orbiter question, with your answer being a question back: entry and exit matter, a landing or takeoff matters, routine orbits don't, "but what would be the logic?"
2. Your idea of an automated run at midnight, everything except the guest-book messages, if the machine can stay on.
3. Your idea of carrying a view into a room: plot the inner planets in the Solar System room, enter the Sun room, and keep them while adding the dust cloud.
4. Piece C's checkbox reading "Keplerian orbit" where the others say hyperbolic or near-parabolic.

Plus one thing Opus owes: checking the OSIRIS-APEX Earth pass of April 2029 against JPL's sheet before building on it.

**Next steps.** A short records close for tonight puts those four on the ledger and resets the page. Then the orbiter ruling, which stands before slice 2's website half because it decides what gets served: more than 230 of the 498 events are Juno, Clipper and BepiColombo circling their planets. Then the website half. Ideas 2 and 3 go on the Deferred list; both are real and neither blocks slice 2.

**On your question, "what would be the logic?"** The path plan already has the piece it needs. It only counts a close pass as a flyby when the spacecraft is inside the body's Hill sphere, the region where that body's gravity beats the Sun's. An orbiter is simply a spacecraft that enters a Hill sphere and doesn't leave. So the rule could be: the first entry into a body's Hill sphere is an event, kept with its fine steps; a departure is an event, kept; everything between, while the spacecraft stays inside, is captured, and in the Solar System room it rides with the planet, no fine steps. A landing is the case where JPL's data ends inside the sphere, so there is an entry and no exit. The loops themselves wait for that planet's room. Nothing is typed by hand; it falls out of the positions the plan already fetches.

Does that rule answer your question, or does it miss a case you have in mind? -- yes that would work well. thanks.

**What it changes:**
- **The comet:** the orrery asks for C/2025 K1 by JPL's permanent number, 1004075, so it draws the right comet again. The pieces still work by their own names; only the record numbers in their notes are corrected.
- **The descriptions:** the four claims nothing supports come out, in the checkbox text and in the longer tooltips too. "Hyperbolic" stays, explained as "an open path around the Sun, not a closed loop." Piece C is called near-parabolic.
- **The records:** your rulings from this evening go into the ledger and the Where We Are page. Two new ledger items: the comet room (L-483) and the check that the website and the orrery agree (L-484).

**Two things I found while building it:**
- **The new number would have cost the comet two drawings.** Two of the orrery's drawing steps only accept objects asked for the old way. Under its new number, C/2025 K1 would have silently lost its two osculating-orbit drawings (the conics drawn at perihelion and at closest approach). The patch fixes both steps. I checked this with the orrery running headlessly: before the fix the comet dropped out of both steps, and after it, it stays in.
- **Your stored copy of the comet's path may hold the wrong comet.** The orrery stores paths by object name, so it won't notice the new number. If the path was stored or extended after JPL handed the old number to the other comet, part of it is the other comet's. I couldn't read the file from here because it's too big to copy over. So the patch reads it on your machine, changes nothing, and compares it with piece B's stored path. **Please paste what it prints under "CACHED PATH CHECK" into the chat.** If it says another body's positions are in there, we clear that entry before you plot the comet.

**A correction to what I told you about eccentricity.** I said e above 1 means the body "leaves for good." That's too strong. JPL's number describes the path at one moment, near the Sun, and the planets' pulls can still change it on the way out. So the new descriptions say "open path" and no longer claim the pieces are escaping the solar system.

Tests: in the sandbox (my cloud copy of the repo), the orrery starts without errors before and after the patch, and the test suites I could run passed. The provenance check is unchanged: 0 on the files the website reads, 276 in the whole tree. The patch also applies cleanly whether or not the ledger tool runs first, and with Windows line endings; a second run refuses. Its one slip in the run so far: the guide file had 62 non-ASCII characters, which the patch now rewrites in plain ASCII.

The rule that a comet whose name also covers its pieces gets asked by JPL's number goes into the horizons-orbital-mechanics skill next session. That skill already took its one update this session.

2. **Commit everything GitHub Desktop lists, then push.** Make sure the list includes: -- b1dcf56be9af1c048140191f15b18f64f9c89498
   - your two run-record copies (`_1140_` and `_1927_`);
   - `COORD_L482_reset_all_rooms_20261024.md`;
   - `HANDOFF_L443_slice2_start_brief_20261024.md`;
   - tonight's two notes, `COORD_L443_comet_span_ruling_20261024.md` and `COORD_L443_words_decisions_20261024.md`;
   - the four moved patches.
3. **Reinstall two skills in Settings > Skills:** interactive-exhibit (1.23) and horizons-orbital-mechanics (1.10). -- yes, and v3.111
4. **On the dashboard, Tools and Caches, run "Path Plan -- preview of slice 2".** It needs the internet and may take about a quarter of an hour. Paste its output into the chat. It writes `data/path_plans_preview.json`; 

    `path_plan.py` works out, for each spacecraft's path, where the drawing needs extra points so the path comes out right. It doesn't draw anything itself. It writes a plan, and later the website (and eventually the orrery) fetches positions from JPL by that plan.

    The problem it solves: most of a spacecraft's path is a gentle curve, and a point every 7 days draws it well. Near a flyby the path bends sharply in a matter of hours. At 7-day spacing that bend gets cut off or drawn as a sharp kink. So the plan says where the flybys are, and how finely to sample around each one.

    For each spacecraft it does five things:

    1. **It finds the dates JPL actually has.** It asks JPL for the spacecraft's position far too early and far too late. JPL refuses and names its first and last dates, and those become the path's span. That is how it learned that the dates typed in the orrery's list have drifted for Juno, Psyche, New Horizons and others.
    2. **It finds the moments the spacecraft comes closest to something.** It fetches the spacecraft's position once a day across that span, and the same for the Sun, the eight planets, the Moon and Pluto. Then it finds each day when the spacecraft is nearer a body than on the days either side.
    3. **It keeps only real flybys.** A near point counts only if it falls inside the body's "Hill sphere", the region where the body's own gravity outweighs the Sun's. That region's size is worked out from the body's mass as JPL publishes it. Without this test the first trial run called a pass 85 million km from Mercury a flyby.
    4. **It pins each flyby down.** It asks JPL again around each flyby, first hour by hour and then minute by minute. That gives the moment of closest approach, the distance and the speed.
    5. **It decides how finely to draw each flyby.** From the distance and speed it works out how sharp the bend is. Then it sets a window around the flyby and a step size within it, using the same rule the orrery already uses for its encounter views: about 30 points through the bend.

    After that it re-checks each plan every 30 days. On most days it asks JPL nothing.

    Running it as the preview does all this for the twelve spacecraft by name, before any of them is set up for the website. It writes the results to `data/path_plans_preview.json`, a file the website never reads. That's also why it takes a while this first time: it fetches daily positions for twelve spacecraft and about a dozen bodies, across decades.

    What I'll do with your output: check the flybys it finds against the ones each mission is known to have made, such as Voyager at Jupiter and Saturn and Juno's Earth flyby. If they line up, the method works on real data and I can build the website half on it. Comets aren't in it yet; they come with that build.

5. commit that with your next push. 

One more thing from your run, before you commit: the one failed check, “Constants change”, is from my patch, and it’s safe to commit past. That check compares constants_new.py with your last commit. It found two comments I edited on piece C’s lines. Those lines name the comet, and “2025” counts as a number to the check, but it can’t read lines shaped like that, so it fails rather than pass them unread. Neither line changes a value; the report itself says 0 values changed. It will pass once you’ve committed.

The plan works on JPL's real data. Every flyby I spot-checked lands on the date of a known mission event, at a sensible distance from the body's centre:

- **Voyager 1:** Jupiter on 1979-03-05 and Saturn on 1980-11-12.
- **Voyager 2:** Saturn, Uranus and Neptune, Neptune at about 29,000 km on 1989-08-25.
- **New Horizons:** Pluto on 2015-07-14, at 13,680 km.
- **Juno:** its Earth flyby on 2013-10-09. **Lucy:** both Earth flybys, plus a Moon pass. **JUICE:** the Moon and Earth on 19 and 20 August 2024, then Venus. **Psyche:** Mars in May 2026. **Europa Clipper:** Mars on 2025-03-01. **BepiColombo:** Earth and Venus.

I'm judging those dates from what I know of the missions; the numbers themselves come from JPL. One event I don't recognise: OSIRIS-APEX passing Earth at 7,526 km on 2029-04-13, the day Apophis passes Earth. I'll check that against JPL's data sheet before building on it.

The finding that needs you is the spacecraft that go into orbit. Of the 498 events, more than 230 are repeated close passes by three spacecraft circling a planet:
- Juno: 110 passes of Jupiter.
- Europa Clipper: 83 passes of Jupiter, in its planned tour.
- BepiColombo: 40 passes of Mercury, mostly after it enters orbit in November.

Each pass would carry its own fine-step window. In the Solar System room those loops are tiny curls around the planet, and they would make up most of the data served.

**My recommendation:** once a spacecraft is in orbit around a planet, the Solar System room shows it riding along with that planet, with no fine steps for each loop. The loops wait for that planet's own room later. This is the same choice already made for asteroid flybys, which belong to the body rooms. The flybys on the way there stay as they are, and so do the passes close to the Sun, like Parker's 42; those are real hairpin bends in the path.

**Do the orbiters ride along with their planet in the Solar System room, with the loops left for the planet rooms?** -- i can see this being an issue. this becomes even more with an orbiter or a lander like the mars lander. i am not sure how to handle this mechanically. the initial entry and exit may be important, a landing, a takeoff, but routine orbits for observations could be omitted. but what would be the logic?

The room should look exactly as it did when it opened. Then press Reset in the Solar System room, which should work as it did before. -- correct. 

-- an idea for consideration. in the orrery, the sun features can be combined with other objects, like say the earth's orbit. in the website we start in the solar system room, but if we then enter the sun room, or the earth room, those views are completely reset. this would also apply to any other sub-rooms, other planets, satellites, encounters, comet breakups, etc. What i am wondering is if we could enable a way to retain a view we create before entering a room and keep it. for example. let's say we are in the solar system and we have the inner planets plotted. and then we move to the sun room, an option to keep the inner planet orbits view when selecting sun features, like the dust cloud. another example, we plot a comet, we enter the comet's room and plot its perihelion encounter, then the roche limit. specific encounter events could be duplicated of course, like perihelion events in the sun room, or close encounter asteroids in the earth room. or spacecraft flybys. so for many events, these duplicates would do the job. the exception would be sun features without sun encounters.

There's one thing for build B to check, because of the minor planets. The drawer's current groups mostly match JPL already, but one of them gathers several of JPL's classes. "Near-Earth asteroids" covers JPL's Atira, Aten, Apollo and Amor classes. Build B will check every body against its JPL class and list any that don't fit their group. Now the orrery edits, in a work copy.

Now the builder fix and its check.

I found two bugs in your run and fixed both. One patch is in each of your folders.

**First bug: the orrery maintenance run said JPL couldn't be reached. That wasn't true.** When the Horizons check asks JPL about a spacecraft in its small-body list, JPL sends back a header with nothing under it. The check took that empty reply for a failed connection, so it stopped at Voyager 1. It now reads the empty reply as "no match." I checked all twelve spacecraft names against JPL this evening, and every one matches.

**Second bug: JPL refused both Voyager paths.** Their data ends at exactly midnight on 1 January 2100. The builder adds 69 seconds to every time it sends JPL, to convert to JPL's clock. So it asked for one minute past the end, and JPL refused. The other ten spacecraft drew fine because their data runs later into their last day. The builder now stops exactly at JPL's last minute.

That bug is why Voyager 1 had no marker, no hover text and no i-panel words: it was drawn from an old copy of its path. Voyager 2 was left out.

**Voyager 1 is now gold**, the same goldenrod as Parker, as you chose.

I tested both patches on fresh copies of the repos, and each refuses a second run. The orrery's check suite passes 66 checks and the website builder's passes 436. Each fix has a check that fails on the old code. The provenance gate still shows 0 Tier-1 findings.
